2. **`vendorId` never validated on PO creation and Direct-Shop-Delivery GRN creation** (`server/routes/inventory.ts`). `POST /purchase-orders` checked `vendorId` for truthiness only; DSD `POST /goods-receipt-notes` (the branch with no linked PO to inherit a checked vendor from) had the same gap. Fixed with an explicit company-ownership check at each site. **Verified live**: a PO-creation attempt with another real company's vendor id now returns `400 "Selected vendor not found for this company."`

3. **`POST /product-warehouses` had no ownership check on `productId`/`warehouseId` at all** (`server/routes/masterEntities.ts`) — despite its own sibling `GET` endpoint and the closely-related `warehouses` routes already being fixed for this exact class in item 96. A user with ordinary `products.update` permission could set stock policy (min/max levels, bin location) against another company's or another branch's warehouse. Fixed with the same company + `branchAccessOk` check pattern used everywhere else.

4. **The *main* `POST /quotations` and `POST /invoices` upsert routes — the ones actually used for both create and edit — were missing the `branchAccessOk` check** that every one of their *dedicated* sibling routes (`PUT /quotations/:id`, `/cancel`, `/convert`, `/invoices/:id/note`, `/paid`, `/cancel`) already had from item 96. A branch-restricted user could edit another branch's quotation/invoice (branch field itself pinned back, but customer/items/amounts/notes freely rewritable) through the one endpoint the isolation model most needed to cover. Fixed by adding the same check to both.

5. **`POST /investors` had no ownership check whatsoever — a complete cross-tenant row hijack, the most severe finding in either pass.** Every other upsert route in this codebase fetches the existing row and calls `assertOwnsRow` before allowing an update; this one didn't, and `investors.id` is a client-supplied primary key with no server default — so `onConflictDoUpdate` on a guessed/known id from another company would silently overwrite that company's investor's name, equity percentage, and capital-contributed total, and reassign `companyId` itself, effectively stealing the record. Fixed with the standard `assertOwnsRow` + `generateId()`-for-new pattern. **Verified live, end to end**: created a real investor as Company A, logged in as a throwaway Company B admin, attempted the exact hijack payload — got `403 Forbidden: this investor belongs to another company`, then confirmed via direct DB read that the investor's name/equity/capital were completely untouched. Test investor and throwaway user deleted afterward.

`tsc --noEmit` clean throughout. Full test suite re-run: 297/316 passing; the 19 failures are the same pre-existing fiscal-month/cross-file-pollution issue already documented in items 93 and 95-96 — reconfirmed by running every flagged file in isolation, where all pass (`employees.test.ts`, `taxReturns.test.ts`, `zatcaWorkflow.test.ts`, `invoicePaidRoute.test.ts` all green alone).

**Where this leaves things**: two independent verification methods (live attack simulation, and a differently-structured code audit) both ran clean against everything already fixed, and the second method's different structure is *why* it caught what the first narrative-style passes didn't. That's meaningfully stronger evidence than another read-through would have been — but items 95, 96, and 97 have now each found real bugs the previous item's audit missed in the same codebase. The honest position is: isolation is significantly better than it was at the start of this session, every specific finding across all three items is verified fixed, and the pattern of "one more pass, one more real bug" means this should be treated as an ongoing property to keep testing for (e.g. the next time a new document-creation route is added, it needs a foreign-key-ownership check by default, not as an afterthought caught by a future audit) rather than a box that gets permanently checked off.

## 98. A Repeatable Structural Check, Not Another One-Off Audit — `scripts/check-data-isolation.mjs`

**Prompted by a direct question**: after item 97, the user asked "can we claim fully achieved till date?" The honest answer given was no — three passes in a row had each found real bugs the previous one(s) missed, all sharing one exact shape, which is evidence the *method* (an LLM reading code and judging it) has a non-zero, unmeasured miss rate, not evidence any specific remaining gap exists. What would actually change that answer is a repeatable, automated check that turns "we looked and didn't find more" into "the codebase can't drift back into this" — proposed and then built, at the user's request, as a real committed tool plus a skill instructing when to run it, rather than left as a one-time cleanup.

**What it does**: `scripts/check-data-isolation.mjs` parses every route handler in `server/routes/*.ts` via the TypeScript compiler API, traces which local variables are (transitively) bound to `req.body` with no database round-trip in between — a real taint-tracking pass, not a regex sweep — and flags any `*Id`-shaped write-payload field whose value traces back to the client with no evidence of a known ownership check (`assertOwnsRow`, `assertDocumentRefsOwnedByCompany`, `assertProductsOwnedByCompany`, `branchAccessOk`, `branchAccessOkViaWarehouse`, `resolveDocumentBranchId`, or a direct scoped `eq(...id...)`+`eq(...companyId...)` pair) nearby. Wired up as `npm run check:isolation` and documented in a new skill, `.claude/skills/data-isolation-guard/SKILL.md`, which instructs running it after any change to `server/routes/*.ts` and states plainly that a clean run is a tripwire result, not a formal proof.

**Getting the signal-to-noise ratio usable took real iteration, documented in the script's own comments**: a first draft flagging every FK-shaped field regardless of its source produced 60 findings against a codebase already verified clean by three audits and a live pen-test — useless as a tool if every run needs 60 manual dismissals. Tightening it to "must trace back to `req.body` specifically" cut that to 27, then a string of principled fixes (a real DB round-trip breaks taint even when a tainted value was used as the lookup key; a trailing `.id` access is always a primary-key self-reference, never a foreign lookup; a fallback/coalesce variable like `targetX = customX || existing.x` is checked against its *original* client-supplied name, not its derived one, via provenance tracking; `router.use()`-level shared middleware gates protect every route after them in the same file; a route whose only door is "must be super-admin" is a deliberate platform-owner exception; two fields — `themeId`, `monthId` — are plain text labels despite the naming convention, confirmed against `schema.ts`'s own comments during the original FK audit) brought it to zero false positives against the known-clean baseline.

**Self-tested before being trusted**: rather than accept "zero findings" as proof the tool works, deliberately stripped a real ownership check back out of a scratch copy of `inventory.ts`'s purchase-order route (reproducing the exact bug already fixed and verified this session) and confirmed the script caught it — twice, in fact, since the first self-test attempt exposed a second, independent real bug in the tool itself (see below) before the reintroduced bug was actually flagged.

**Building and self-testing the tool found two more real, previously-undiscovered bugs** — a second concrete demonstration that this class of gap keeps reappearing and a repeatable check earns its keep:
1. **`POST /quotations/:id/convert` trusted a client-supplied `createdById` outright** (`server/routes/transactions.ts`) — every other document-creation route in this file sets `createdById` from `req.user.id`; this one destructured it straight from `req.body` and inserted it verbatim onto the resulting invoice. Let any caller misattribute a converted invoice's authorship to an arbitrary user id, including one from a different company — an audit-trail integrity gap, not a data-exposure one, but real. Fixed: `createdById` is now always `req.user.id`, matching every sibling route.
2. **`POST /purchase-returns`'s line items had no explicit `productId` ownership check** (`server/routes/inventory.ts`) — found only after fixing a real gap in the checker itself (it wasn't resolving a `.values(itemRows)` call back to `itemRows`'s own declaration, meaning it couldn't see inside *any* bulk-insert built via an intermediate variable — a blind spot that likely also hid other findings during earlier passes, not just this one). In practice a cross-company `productId` here was already indirectly blocked by the existing received-quantity-vs-returned-quantity check (a product never actually received on the referenced GRN has zero "available to return," rejecting any positive-quantity line) — but that's a side effect of unrelated business-rule math, not an explicit check, and doesn't cover a zero-quantity line. Fixed with the same `assertProductsOwnedByCompany` call used at every other item-insertion site.

**Verified**: `tsc --noEmit` clean; full test suite re-run, 301/316 passing, remainder reconfirmed as the same pre-existing fiscal-month/cross-file-pollution issue in isolation (all pass alone); the `createdById` fix confirmed by direct code/type review (a one-line, obviously-correct change mirroring 8+ already-verified sibling call sites); the `purchase-returns` `productId` fix verified live end-to-end — a cross-company product id now returns `400 "One or more selected products were not found for this company."` `node scripts/check-data-isolation.mjs` itself now exits 0 against the current codebase.

**What this does and doesn't settle**: every specific gap found across four rounds (items 95-98) is now fixed and verified at least two ways. The script closes the *exact* pattern behind all of them going forward — a new route or a new field can't reintroduce this specific shape of bug without the check catching it (assuming it's run, which the skill exists to prompt). It does not, and cannot, prove no *other* shape of isolation bug exists — that would need the same escalating skepticism applied to whatever the next category turns out to be, exactly as items 95 through 97 each did for one another.

## 99. The Test Suite's Own Flakiness — Finally Fixed, Two Distinct Root Causes

**Prompted directly**: after being told the full suite's remaining 17-19 failures were "the same pre-existing fiscal-month/cross-file flakiness, verified passing in isolation" for the fifth time across items 93 and 95-98, the user asked plainly whether it needed fixing. It did — that exact sentence had become a crutch, repeated across five separate items without ever being acted on, and it was actively making it harder to tell a real regression from noise.

**Root cause #1 — username collisions, finally traced to its actual mechanism** (already correctly diagnosed once, in item 89's discovery, but never acted on beyond that one file): 29 of the 30 test files build throwaway usernames via `someId.slice(0, 8)`. `generateId()` produces UUIDv7s (`src/id.ts`) — by design, the **first 8 hex characters are a big-endian millisecond timestamp, not random data** (the actual randomness starts later, in bytes 6-15). Truncating to those first 8 characters for a "unique-looking" suffix is close to the opposite of unique: two test files whose fixtures happen to run in the same millisecond (routine under vitest's default parallel file execution) produce identical usernames. Since `users.username` has no database uniqueness constraint, the second insert doesn't fail — it silently creates a second row with the same login name, and whichever one a subsequent login resolves to determines which company's session an entirely unrelated test ends up running against. **Fixed**: dropped the `.slice(0, 8)` truncation across all 29 files (mechanical, one pattern, one fix) — every generated identifier now uses the full UUID as its uniqueness source.

**Root cause #2 — a real unscoped `DELETE`, not timing at all**: fixing #1 didn't fully clear the suite — it dropped the failure count from ~19 to a much smaller, *consistent* set, which was the first real signal this was two separate bugs stacked on each other, not one. The remaining failures were 100% deterministic (`"Transaction date 2026-09-02 does not fall within any defined fiscal month"`), always in `zatcaWorkflow.test.ts`/`taxReturns.test.ts`, always passing alone — the opposite of what genuine wall-clock date-rollover flakiness would look like (that would be intermittent, tied to when the suite happened to run relative to midnight, not tied to which *other* files were in the batch). That inconsistency between the symptom and the "date rollover" explanation is what prompted a fresh look rather than accepting the label again. Found in `tests/permissionCrudModel.test.ts:398`: a cleanup line

```ts
await db.delete(schema.fiscalMonths).where(eq(schema.fiscalMonths.id, '2026-09'));
```

`fiscalMonths` is one of exactly two tables in this schema with a **composite** primary key (`id`, `companyId` together) — `id` alone (`"2026-09"`) is not unique across companies, unlike every other table's plain UUID primary key (confirmed by sweeping every other `delete(...).where(eq(...id...))` call across all 30 files — all of them target genuine single-column UUID primary keys, safe by construction). This one line deleted **every company's** row for the current calendar month, including whichever throwaway companies `zatcaWorkflow.test.ts`/`taxReturns.test.ts` had just set up and were mid-test with, the instant this specific `permissionCrudModel.test.ts` test ran in the same batch. **Fixed**: scoped the delete to `and(eq(id, '2026-09'), eq(companyId, companyId))` — this test's own company only, exactly matching the pattern already used correctly in every other cleanup in the suite.

**Verified**: full suite (`npx vitest run`) run three consecutive times after both fixes — **316/316 passing, 30/30 files, every single run**, zero flakiness. `tsc --noEmit` clean throughout (both the app code touched earlier in this session and all 29 edited test files).

**Why this took two passes to actually fix, not just describe**: the first fix (usernames) was correctly identified back in item 89 but left as "a separate follow-up task" and then re-described as an open problem four more times without being executed. The second bug (the unscoped delete) was *invisible* until the first one was fixed — it was masked underneath a noisier, higher-frequency failure mode the whole time. This is the same lesson as items 95-98's isolation work: a bug report that gets re-labeled "known, pre-existing, unrelated" across multiple sessions is worth actually re-opening rather than continuing to cite, especially once the specific mechanism can be named precisely rather than gestured at.

## 100. TO DO — Quick-Add Product/Vendor Modal on Transactional Forms (proposed, not yet built)

**Status: proposed only, nothing implemented.** Unlike every other item in this log, this is a forward-looking request, not a completed piece of work — recorded here at the user's request so it isn't lost, to be picked up in a future session.

**The ask**: allow adding a new Product (and Vendor/Customer) directly from wherever it's selected on a transactional document form (Invoice, Quotation, PO, GRN, Expense, etc.) via an inline "+" quick-add modal, instead of the current flow of abandoning the in-progress document, navigating to the full Product/Vendor/Customer master screen, creating it there, and coming back.

**Proposed structure** (discussed with the user, not yet built):
1. **A genuinely separate, minimal quick-add modal — not the full Master Entity form reused in a dialog.** The full forms in `MasterEntities.tsx` carry many fields (categories, warehouse links, images, min/max stock levels, barcodes...) that are noise mid-invoice. A quick-add modal should ask only for what the backend actually requires (e.g. name/type/price for a product; name/phone-or-email/buyer-type for a vendor or customer), with sensible defaults for everything else — the user can flesh out the rest later from the real master screen.
2. **Reuse the exact same backend routes** (`POST /api/products`, `POST /api/vendors`, `POST /api/customers`) — same validation, same company scoping, same permission checks (`products.create`, `vendors.create`, etc.) as the full forms. This is a new UI entry point onto existing, already-secured routes, not a new code path.
3. **Build it once as a reusable component**, e.g. `<QuickAddProductButton onCreated={(product) => ...} />` — a self-contained "+" trigger + its own modal + the API call, taking a callback that hands back the newly-created row. Every form that needs this (Invoice, Quotation, PO, GRN, Expense, POS) wires the same component in rather than each screen inventing its own version.
4. **State flow**: on success, merge the new row into `db` via the existing `onUpdateDbLocal` pattern (visible in every dropdown app-wide immediately), auto-select it in the specific line/field that triggered the quick-add, and leave every other already-typed field on the in-progress document completely untouched.
5. **Permission-gate the "+" button itself** the same way the rest of the app already does (`can('products.create')`, `can('vendors.create')`) — hide it rather than show-then-fail server-side.

**Open question, not yet resolved with the user**: whether "vendor" in the original request meant the purchase-side forms (PO/Expense/GRN, which actually reference vendors) specifically, or whether "product/customer" on the Invoice form itself was intended and "vendor" was a slip — or both. Confirm scope before starting implementation.

## 101. Unsaved-Changes Guard, Session-Lifetime Fix (Real Ghost-Row Bug Found), and Admin Session Visibility/Revocation (Sep 2026)

Triggered by a live user report bundling three concerns: (1) leaving an open Invoice/Quotation/Expense form for any other screen silently discarded in-progress work with no warning; (2) a session created one day was still valid the next day after fully closing the browser, raising the question of whether sessions expire at all; (3) no way to see who is currently logged in or forcibly end someone's session.

**1. Unsaved-changes navigation guard.** New `useDirtyGuard(currentValues)` hook (`src/hooks.ts`) generalizes the snapshot-vs-current dirty-check `AdminSettings.tsx`'s Canvas Designer already used for its own `hasUnsavedCanvasChanges`. Wired into `InvoiceModule`/`QuotationModule`/`ExpenseModule`'s create (and, for Quotation, edit) forms via an `onDirtyChange` callback prop, consumed by `App.tsx`'s `handleNavigate` — the one choke point nearly every navigation action (sidebar clicks, every form's Cancel/Done handler) already funnels through. Also closed the one confirmed bypass (`PosModule`'s `onClose` called `setActiveTab` directly, skipping the guard) and added a `beforeunload` listener for real tab-close/refresh. A real synchronization subtlety: each form's baseline snapshot must be built from the same *local* values used to auto-populate defaults (date, default customer/tax/bank, primary branch), not the React state variables, which haven't updated yet within that same synchronous effect — using the state variables would have made every fresh form read as "dirty" the instant it opened. Deliberately excludes `formWarehouseId` (Invoice) from tracking — it's re-derived by a second, separate effect racing the snapshot. Deliberately clears the dirty flag *synchronously* right before navigating away after a successful save (not via the `isDirty`-change effect, which fires too late and would wrongly re-prompt immediately after a successful save). Scoped to the three highest-traffic forms for this pass; remaining Inventory/Procurement forms get the same mechanical treatment as a follow-up.

**2. Session-lifetime fix — and a real, unrelated bug found along the way.** Root cause of "the session never expired": connect-pg-simple's `PgSession` had no `ttl` option (silently defaulting to an undocumented 24h) and the cookie had no `maxAge` at all — an accidental, invisible policy. Fixed with an explicit, coordinated `SESSION_TTL_SECONDS` (env-configurable via `app.secrets`, default 7 days) applied to both. While tracing the non-cookie (`x-session-id`) auth path — the app's actual primary auth mechanism, sourced from `localStorage`, not the cookie — found that `isAuthenticated` was writing `userId`/`companyId` onto a fresh, disconnected `req.session` object on *every single header-authenticated request*, which express-session interprets as "modified" and persists as a brand-new throwaway row. Confirmed live: **4,869 accumulated ghost rows** in the local dev `user_sessions` table. Fixing this required real care: that same write is also how `req.targetCompanyId` resolution (server.ts) sees a super-admin's `POST /api/switch-company` selection on the very next line of the *same request* — see [item 64](#64-critical-fix-super-admin-company-selection-was-never-actually-honored--apistate-returned-every-tenants-data-unfiltered-aug-2026), which fixed exactly this mechanism once already. Naively deleting the write would have silently reintroduced item 64's bug. Fixed by replacing the `req.session` write with a same-request-only local variable (`sessionCompanyIdOverride`), read by the target-company resolution alongside `req.session?.companyId` — identical resolution behavior, zero new session-store writes. Also added an explicit, throttled sliding-expiration touch (extends `expire` once past the halfway point of the TTL, not on every request) — implemented manually rather than via express-session's `rolling` option, which only engages for the cookie flow and would never fire for header-based requests at all.

**3. Admin session visibility + revocation.** Additive `userId`/`companyId`/`lastActivity` columns on `user_sessions` (`onDelete: cascade` — a session for a deleted user/company is meaningless; without cascade, deleting a user with any live session throws a FK violation instead of succeeding, confirmed live against several existing tests' own cleanup). New `server/routes/sessions.ts`: `GET /api/admin/sessions` (admin-tier only, not a delegable permission leaf — same class as Roles/DB Backup) lists active, non-ghost sessions (`expire > now() AND userId IS NOT NULL`), company-scoped unless super-admin; `DELETE /api/admin/sessions/:sid` revokes one, rejecting a cross-company target and rejecting revoking your own current session (with a clear "use logout instead" message). New `AdminSettings.tsx` "Active Sessions" tab (System & Backup group) with a live table and per-row Revoke button. A revoked session isn't pushed offline (no websockets in this app) — it simply fails `isAuthenticated`'s existing checks on that user's very next request.

**Verified**: `tsc --noEmit` and `check:isolation` clean throughout. New `tests/sessionLifecycle.test.ts` (12 tests, real HTTP + real Postgres, no mocks): TTL enforcement, sliding extension, the ghost-row fix (scoped to a specific test user's own rows via the `sess` JSON blob — a *global* row-count assertion proved flaky under vitest's concurrent test-file execution against the shared dev DB, since other files' unrelated logins move the count independently), the company-switch regression specifically (switch company, make repeated `x-session-id` requests, assert both the correct company keeps resolving *and* no ghost rows appear — the exact intersection the fix touches), and the new list/revoke routes' permission and cross-company isolation boundaries. `tests/companyScoping.test.ts` (item 64's own regression suite) re-run unmodified and still green. Full suite **357/357** (345 prior + 12 new). Manually verified live: created a throwaway session in one browser tab, confirmed it appeared in another tab's admin session list scoped correctly, revoked it via the real API, confirmed the first tab was bounced to the login screen on its next action; separately confirmed the unsaved-changes prompt fires on an Invoice form with a typed note, blocks navigation on Cancel, and does *not* fire after a successful save. New strings translated (real Arabic/Urdu, not the auto-translate button, per this project's `ux-translation` convention).

### Critical files
- `src/hooks.ts` — `useDirtyGuard`
- `src/App.tsx` — `handleNavigate` guard, `beforeunload`, PosModule bypass fix
- `src/components/InvoiceModule.tsx`, `QuotationModule.tsx`, `ExpenseModule.tsx` — guard wiring
- `server.ts` — TTL config, ghost-row fix, sliding-expiration touch, login/switch-company backfill
- `src/db/schema.ts` — `user_sessions.userId`/`companyId`/`lastActivity`
- `server/routes/sessions.ts` (new)
- `src/components/AdminSettings.tsx` — "Active Sessions" tab
- `tests/sessionLifecycle.test.ts` (new)

---

## 102. Super-Admin "No Active Fiscal Month" on a Restored Session — Stale Seed `selectedCompanyId`, Plus Removing the Fragile `?companyId=` Pattern It Exposed (Sep 2026)

Triggered by a live user report while reviewing item 101: logging in as a super-admin showed "No Active Fiscal Month Detected" despite a real open month existing, and explicitly switching companies "fixed" it. Root-caused, not guessed — reproduced and un-reproduced live (see below).

**Root cause**: `dbStore.ts`'s `INITIAL_DB.selectedCompanyId` was hardcoded to `SEED_COMPANIES[0].id` (a long-standing leftover demo-company id that happens to still be a real company in this deployment, "CNC Woodcraft & Design"). `App.tsx`'s post-login state merge only overwrites a super-admin's `selectedCompanyId` with their real home company `if (!isSuperAdminUser || !selectedCompanyId)` — and since the seed placeholder is always truthy, that check silently never fired for a super-admin. This only affected the **session-restore path** (an existing `sessionUserId` found in `localStorage` on page load/reload — exactly the scenario item 101's session-lifetime work is about), not a fresh login-form submission, which has its own separate, unconditional assignment (`LoginScreen`'s `onLoginSuccess` in `App.tsx`) and was never affected. The bulk `/api/state` payload itself was always correctly scoped server-side (that fetch carries no `?companyId=`) — but `getActiveOpenMonth`/`getOpenMonths` (`dbStore.ts`) filter that already-correct data by the client-side `db.selectedCompanyId`, which stayed silently pinned to the seed company forever once set. Also visibly reverted the sidebar branding/active-org display to the stale seed company on every reload. **Fixed** by changing `INITIAL_DB.selectedCompanyId` to `''` — restoring the merge's own stated intent ("only pin to their assigned company on first load").

**Verified live, both directions**: reproduced with the old hardcoded value (dashboard showed "No Active Fiscal Month", branding reverted to "CNC Woodcraft & Design") on a page reload while logged in as a super-admin whose real home company (with real open months) was a different company; re-verified fixed after restoring the empty-string default, same reload, same account. `tsc --noEmit`, `check:isolation`, and the full suite (357/357) all clean throughout.

**Related hardening, from the same review**: several components manually appended a redundant `?companyId=${db.selectedCompanyId}` on fetches whose server routes already resolve the correct company from the session (`req.targetCompanyId`) — safe today only because each is an unmemoized function re-evaluated every render, but exactly the shape of bug [item 64](#64-critical-fix-super-admin-company-selection-was-never-actually-honored--apistate-returned-every-tenants-data-unfiltered-aug-2026) once was: a future `useCallback`/memoization on any of these would silently freeze a stale company id, and since an explicit `?companyId=` always outranks the session in `isAuthenticated`'s resolution order, that stale value would win forever. Removed the redundant param from every call site found (server already ignores it in favor of `req.targetCompanyId`, confirmed no route reads `req.query.companyId` directly): `InvoiceModule.tsx`, `QuotationModule.tsx` (4 call sites), `Dashboard.tsx`, `PosModule.tsx`, `AdminSettings.tsx` (3 call sites), `MasterEntities.tsx` (2 call sites). Behavior-neutral — confirmed via the full test suite plus a live walkthrough.

### Critical files
- `src/dbStore.ts` — `INITIAL_DB.selectedCompanyId` fix
- `src/components/InvoiceModule.tsx`, `QuotationModule.tsx`, `Dashboard.tsx`, `PosModule.tsx`, `AdminSettings.tsx`, `MasterEntities.tsx` — redundant `?companyId=` removed
